Subdivisions (a) and (g) were amended; subdivisions (b), (c), (d), (e), and (f) remain unchanged. 73.01, infra.

Subdivision (a). The changes effected by the 1946 amendments can best be explained from a reprint (old matter stricken out is in brackets, new matter is in italics): "(a) When and How Taken. When an appeal is permitted by law from a district court to a circuit court of appeals [and within the time prescribed, a] the time within which an appeal may be taken shall be 30 days from the entry of the judgment appealed from unless a shorter time is provided by law, except that in any action in which the United States or an officer or agency thereof is a party the time as to all parties shall be 60 days from such entry, and except that upon a showing of excusable neglect based on a failure of a party to learn of the entry of the judgment the district court in any action may extend the time for appeal not exceeding 30 days from the expiration of the original time herein prescribed. The running of the time for appeal is terminated by a timely motion made pursuant to any of the rules hereinafter enumerated, and the full time for appeal fixed in this subdivision commences to run and is to be computed from the entry of any of the following orders made upon a timely motion under such rules: granting or denying a motion for judgment under Rule 50(b); or granting or denying a motion under Rule 52(b) to amend or make additional findings of fact, whether or not an alteration of the judgment would be required if the motion is granted; or granting or denying a motion under Rule 59 to alter or amend the judgment; or denying a motion for a new trial under Rule 59.

¶ 0.01 

"A party may appeal from a judgment by filing with the district court a notice of appeal. Failure of the appellant to take any of the further steps to secure the review of the judgment appealed from does not affect the validity of the appeal, but is ground only for such remedies as are specified in this rule or, when no remedy is specified, for such action as the appellate court deems appropriate, which may include dismissal of the appeal. If an appeal has not been docketed, the parties, with the approval of the district court, may dismiss the appeal by stipulation filed in that court, or that court may dismiss the appeal upon motion and notice by the appellant.” 

The amendments do three things: (1) prescribe the time within which an appeal to a circuit court of appeals may be taken that, in general, is shorter than formerly prevailed; (2) prescribe the effect upon the appeal time of the making of certain motions that affect the finality of a judgment; and (3) allow the dismissal of an appeal by the district court under certain circumstances, where the appeal has not yet been docketed with the circuit court of appeals. (1) Time for appeal under 1946 amendments. The most important amendment of subdivision (a) is the statement of the time within which an appeal may be taken. Rule 73(a), as originally promulgated, merely provided that an appeal could be taken "within the time prescribed" by law. As pointed out in the Comment under Rule 54, supra, the historic practice in the federal courts in civil actions, at law and in equity, has been that a final judgment is a prerequisite to an appeal, subject to certain statutory exceptions, and amended Rule 54(b) as well as its predecessor, original Rule 54(b), adapted and continued that practice. And the general maximum time for an appeal from a district court to the circuit court of appeals from a final judgment has been three months from the entry of the judgment. 28 USC § 230. Common statutory exceptions allowing interlocutory appeals, within 30 days from the entry of the interlocutory order, are to be found in 28 USC § 227 (appeal from an interlocutory order granting or denying an injunction, or an interlocutory order appointing a receiver or refusing to wind up a pending receivership), § 227a ("When in any suit in equity for the infringement of letters patent for inventions, a decree is rendered which is final except for the ordering of an accounting, an appeal may be taken from such decree to the Circuit Court of Appeals. ."). In the District of Columbia, by special rule authorized by Act of Congress, the time for taking an appeal in an ordinary case was long fixed at 20 days from the date of the entry of the judgment. This time was eventually enlarged to 30 days. See Hill v. Hawes (1944) 320 US 520, 64 S Ct 334, 88 L ed 283, 149 ALR 736. As stated, the

Federal Rules as originally promulgated made no change in the statutory time periods for appeal. The change in the time for appeal to a circuit court of appeals by the 1946 amendment was taken pursuant to recommendations of the Judicial Conference of Senior Circuit Judges made in 1944 and 1945. Since in the main the general time for appeal was three months, at least as to final judgments, the appeal time has been considerably reduced in line with developments in state appellate practice. No hardship is worked, since all that is necessary to take an appeal under the Rule is to file a notice of appeal with the district court.

¶ 0.140 

(a) The general time within which to take an appeal in cases which, for convenience, may be designated as private cases is 30 days from the entry of the judgment, whether the judgment is interlocutory or final, unless a shorter time is provided by law. The only situation in which such a shorter time is provided seems to be that of 45 USC § 159, allowing an appeal within 10 days from the judgment of the district court upon an award of a board of arbitration under the Railway Labor Act. By virtue of Rule 81(a)(3), appeals in these cases are governed by the Federal Rules. (b) In an action in which the United States or an officer or agency thereof is a party, which for convenience may be described as government cases, the time for appeal is 60 days from the entry of the judgment, whether interlocutory or final, and whether the judgment is in favor of or against the government. Allowance of 60 days to the government, its officers and agencies, within which to take an appeal is justified by the necessary routine of the case through the hands of various governmental officers in determining whether the case is one justifying an appeal. And since the Committee felt that it would be unjust to allow the United States, its officers or agencies extra time yet deny it to other parties in the case, and because of the difficulty that might ensue in the case of cross-appeals governed by different time periods, amended Rule 73 (a) uniformly gives all parties in government cases 60 days. The term "officer" is defined in Rule 81 (f) to include "a collector of internal revenue, a former collector of internal revenue, or the personal representative of a deceased collector of internal revenue". (c) The applicable time period for appeal commences to run from the entry of a judgment as provided in Rule 58 and, as pointed out in the Comment thereunder, notice of the entry of a judgment is not necessary to start the appeal time running. While the clerk is required by Rule 77 (d) to give notice by mail of the entry of the judgment, this duty is a mere accommodation service and failure on his part to perform his duty or, if performed, failure of the losing party to receive notice does not directly affect the time for appeal. But amended Rule 73 (a) does take into account the situation where a party fails to learn of the entry of the judgment within time to appeal, and provides that in this case "upon a showing of excusable neglect the district court in any action may extend the time for appeal not exceeding 30 days from the expiration of the original time herein prescribed." 

The Committee in its 1946 Note states its reasons for this provision as follows: 

"The existing law has provided that the time runs from the date of entry of the judgment and not from the date of notice, and this rule is preserved in the proposed amendments, except that some regard is given to the failure to receive notice of the entry of judgment by providing that an additional thirty days may be allowed if a party fails to appeal within the original thirty or sixty days as the case may be, because of excusable neglect based on his failure to learn of the entry of the judgment. In Rule 77 is a provision requiring the clerk to mail notice to all parties of the entry of an order or judgment. That rule is a reiteration of an old equity rule, and the service rendered by the clerk under that rule and the old equity rule was a mere accommodation service and not intended to affect the running of the time for appeal. 

Yet, in Hill v. Hawes (1944) 320 US 520 originating in the District of 

Columbia, when only twenty days from the entry of judgment was the period

¶ 0.01 

allowed for taking. an appeal and the clerk failed to send this formal notice, the district judge relieved the party by vacating the judgment and reentering it, so that the time started anew from the reentry. This action was sustained by the Supreme Court. At a time when the court lost jurisdiction of the cause at the expiration of a term, the holding in Hill v. Hawes would have caused no difficulty, but since Rule 6 of these rules abolishes the old doctrine that the expiration of a term ends the court's jurisdiction, the effect of the decision in Hill v. Hawes seemed to be that at any time, even long after the entry of judgment, the court might vacate it for the purpose of reentering it and thus reviving the right of appeal. The proposed amendment of Rule 

73(a) allows the sort of relief that was brought about in Hill v. Hawes, but avoids the difficulty of indefinite lack of finality of the judgment, by providing that the extension of the time for appeal as the result of excusable neglect for failing to receive notice of it must be limited to an additional thirty days. 

The party in whose favor the judgment is rendered may, as provided in Rule 77, himself serve a formal notice on the defeated party of the entry of the judgment and thus avoid the possibility of any extension of time for appeal under the amendment of Rule 73(a)." In this connection see the 

Comment under Rule 5, supra. (2) Effect of timely motion upon appeal time. The second sentence of the amended rule deals with this problem. It should be noted that the time for appeal is terminated only by a timely motion made for judgment n.o.v. under 

Rule 50(b), or to amend or make additional findings under Rule 52(b), or for a new trial under Rule 59(b), or to alter or amend the judgment under 

Rule 59(e). To be timely a motion under Rule 52(b), 59(b), or 59 (e) must be made not later than 10 days after the entry of judgment; and a motion for judgment n.o.v. under Rule 50(b) must be made within 10 days after the reception of a verdict, or if a verdict is not returned, within 10 days after the jury has been discharged. In general then, all of these rules provide for a very short time, a 10-day period. And it should be recalled that under 

Rule 6(b) as amended, the court may not enlarge any of these time periods. 

This changes the former practice since under original Rule 6(b) the only express limitation upon the court's power to enlarge any of these time periods was a prohibition against enlarging the time for moving for a new trial under Rule 59. See Comment under Rule 52, supra, where it is pointed out that in so far as the case of Leishman v. Associated Wholesale Electric Co. (1943) 

318 US 203, 63 S Ct 543, 87 L ed 714, held that Rule 6(b), as originally promulgated, allowed the district court to enlarge the time to make a motion for amended findings, the principle has been obviated by the 1946 amendments to Rule 6(b). The philosophy of amended Rule 6(b) is that since the effect of the expiration of a term of court has been eliminated there must be some limitation upon the court's power to deal with a judgment in the interest of finality. In keeping with that philosophy amended Rule 73 (a) requires that the motions enumerated therein must be made timely if they are to toll the running of the time for appeal. In establishing this principle, the Committee consciously avoided the rule established in bankruptcy proceedings and chose the rule formerly prevailing at law and in equity and under the betterdecided cases under the Federal Rules. On this matter the Committee stated in its Note of 1946 as follows:

¶ 0.142 

"In bankruptcy proceedings it is established that as the bankruptcy court has no terms it has the power at any time for good reason to revise its judgments or orders upon seasonable application and before rights have vested on the faith of its action. A motion so to do may be entertained even after the expiration of time for appeal, and such appeal time will start running anew upon the disposition of the motion. Wayne United Gas 

Co. v. Owens-Illinois Glass Co. (1937) 300 US 131; Bowman v. Loperena 

(1940) 311 US 262; Pfister v. Northern Illinois Finance Corp. (1942) 317 

US 144; Chapman v. Federal Land Bank (CCA6th, 1941) 117 F2d 321;

State of Missouri v. Todd (CCA8th, 1941) 122 F2d 804. In ordinary civil actions governed by the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure, however, the better view is that when the time limits prescribed in the rules expire, the court loses its jurisdiction to entertain a motion, as for new trial or for a rehearing or to vacate or amend, as the case may be, and cannot thereafter entertain such a motion and thereby start the appeal time running anew. 

Safeway Stores, Inc. v. Coe (App DC 1943) 136 F2d 771; Jusino v. Morales & Tio (CCA1st, 1944) 139 F2d 946; Nealon v. Hill (CCA9th, 1945) 149 F 

2d 883; Norris v. Camp (CCA10th, 1944) 144 F2d 1. It has been said that the bankruptcy rule, stated supra, is to be distinguished as based on the distinctive nature of bankruptcy proceedings; and that since the Federal 

Rules have abolished terms and substituted therefor various definite time limits, the same rule should be applied when such time limits expire as was applied formerly when terms were effective. Safeway Stores, Inc. v. Coe, supra. See also discussion of these distinctions in Oglebay, Some Developments in Bankruptcy Law (1946) 20 J of Nat'l Ass'n of Ref 76, 80. "Prior to the adoption of the Federal Rules the term of court played an all-important role in the district court's power over its final judgments at law and in equity. While during the term the district court had plenary power over such judgments, it was in general without power to reconsider its final judgments at law and in equity after the expiration of the term, unless (1) the proceeding seeking relief was begun within the term, or (2) the court, during the term, reserved control over the judgment and the proceeding seeking relief was begun within that extended period. See Delaware 

L. & W. R. Co. v. Rellstab (1928) 276 US 1 (law); In re Metropolitan Trust 

Co. (1910) 218 US 321 (equity); United States v. Mayer (1914) 235 US 55 (law-criminal); Zimmern v. United States (1936) 298 US 167 (equity).

The exception to the general rule just stated was the utilization, under certain circumstances, of the ancillary remedies of audita querela, coram nobis, coram vobis, bill of review and bill in the nature of review-remedies which grew up to give relief after term time in certain limited and defined situations. 

Under the proposed amendment to Rule 6(b) the court may not enlarge the time for taking action under Rule 50 (b), 52(b), 59(b), (d) and (e), and 60 (b); and the time periods of these rules limit the court's power just as effectively as the term time, which they replace, formerly did. See Moore and Rogers, Federal Relief from Civil Judgments (1946) 55 Yale LJ 623, 627-630, 685-693. "Rulings or dicta to the contrary, as in United States v. Schlotfeldt (CCA 

7th, 1943) 136 F2d 935; Babler v. United States (CCA8th, 1943) 137 F2d 

98 (dictum); Suggs v. Mutual Benefit Health & Accident Ass'n (CCA10th,

¶ 0.01 

1940) 115 F2d 80 (dictum), are not acceptable in light of these considerations."

If the motion is timely made the full time for appeal commences to run anew and is to be computed from the entry of an order granting or denying a motion under Rules 50 (b), 52(b), and 59(e); and from an order denying a motion for a new trial under Rule 59 (b). If the court grants a motion for a new trial made by a party under Rule 59(b), or grants a new trial on its own initiative under Rule 59(d) then there is, of course, no judgment to appeal from and the time for appeal does not commence to run until the entry of a new judgment following the new trial.

As pointed out in the Comment to Rule 72, supra, the Leishman case recognized that a motion to amend or make additional findings of fact in more than purely formal or mechanical aspects tolls the running of the time for appeal. Since it is difficult to distinguish between a motion raising only formal or mechanical matters and one going to substance, and since the motion under Rule 52(b) to be timely must be made within the ten day period, Rule 73(a) in the interest of certainty provides that the motion under Rule 52(b) tolls the running of the appeal time "whether or not an alteration of the judgment would be required if the motion is granted". 

This provision then goes beyond and settles a matter not expressly decided in the Leishman case.

Amended Rule 73(a) did not include within its enumeration a motion for relief from a judgment or order under Rule 60 (b), since that rule expressly provides that a motion made thereunder "does not affect the finality of a judgment or suspend its operation". 

(3) Dismissal of Appeal.—The sentence added at the end of Rule 73(a) 

gives the district court express power to dismiss an appeal on stipulation of the parties or upon appellant's motion before the appeal has been docketed with the circuit court of appeals. This changes the former practice, since the general view had been that once the appeal was taken the district court had no authority to proceed further in the matter except in aid of the appeal or to correct clerical mistakes under Rule 60 (a), until it had received the mandate of the appellate court. This change in practice avoids what is often a useless formality and expense of docketing the appeal and then dismissing it in the appellate court. 

Power of appellate court to extend time for docketing appeal and filing record. See under subdivision (g), infra.

Power to grant substantive relief from an interlocutory order.-Rule 60 (b) does not restrict the district court's power to reconsider the merits of an interlocutory order. See Comment under Rule 60, supra. But for the reasons stated supra there should be no reconsideration of an interlocutory order for the purpose of extending the time for appeal. 

Subdivision (g). This subdivision was amended in the following particulars (old matter stricken out is in brackets, new matter is in italics): "(g) Docketing and Record on Appeal. The record on appeal as provided for in Rules 75 and 76 shall be filed with the appellate court and the [action] appeal there docketed within 40 days from the date of filing the notice of appeal; except that, when more than one appeal is taken from

¶ 0.

144 the same judgment to the same appellate court, the district court may prescribe the time for filing and docketing, which in no event shall be less than 40 days from the date of filing the first notice of appeal. In all cases the district court in its discretion and with or without motion or notice may extend the time for filing the record on appeal and docketing the [action] appeal, if its order for extension is made before the expiration of the period for filing and docketing as originally prescribed or as extended by a previous order; but the district court shall not extend the time to a day more than 

90 days from the date of filing the first notice of appeal."

Substitution of the word "appeal" for the less exact word "action" is verbal.

Subdivision (g), as originally promulgated, had inadvertently required the record on appeal to be filed and the appeal to be docketed within a stated time from the date of the notice of appeal. The mere date of the notice of appeal should properly be wholly immaterial. Under Rule 73(a), both as originally promulgated and as amended, an appeal to a circuit court of appeals is taken by filing a notice of appeal with the district court. And 

Rule 75 (g), which specifies the contents of the record on appeal, provides that the notice of appeal with date of filing shall be included in the appellate record. In recognition of these facts subdivision (g) has been amended to make the time periods specified therein run from the date of filing the notice of appeal. 

Under Rule 6(b), as amended, the district court may not extend the time stated in Rule 73 (g), except to the extent and under the conditions stated in it. The limitation upon the power of the district court to extend the time for docketing the appeal and filing the record on appeal with the circuit court of appeals does not, however, limit the power of the appellate court to extend the time for docketing and filing. Pyramid Motor Freight Corp. v. Ispass (1947) US, 67 S Ct 954, 91 L ed In this case appellant did not seek any extension of time from the district court for docketing the appeal and filing the record on appeal as permitted by Rule 73(g). After the time stated in this rule had run, appellant sought and was granted an extension of time by a single judge of the Second Circuit for docketing and filing, with which he complied. The Second Circuit thereafter denied appellee's motion to dismiss the appeal under Rule 73 (a). Held, affirmed. Mr. Justice Burton stated: "The authority of a Judge of the Circuit Court of Appeals for the 

Second Circuit to extend the time for filing the record on appeal appears to be supported by Rule 15 of that court. That Rule, however, was not discussed by counsel and we sustain the action taken by the Circuit Court of Appeals under authority of Rule 73(a), even without reference to its own 

Rule 15. "The principal argument against the final action of the Circuit Court of Appeals on this motion is based upon the following statement in that court's opinion: 'In the case at bar there was no abuse of discretion in extending the time, despite the somewhat feeble excuses for delay, since the appeal presents a substantial question as to the correctness of the judgment.' (Italics supplied.) 2 Cir, 152 F2d 619, 621. It is urged that this shows that the court based its refusal to dismiss the appeal on the substantiality of the

¶ 0.01 

question to be presented on the merits of the appeal, rather than on the substantiality of the excuses for the delay in filing the record. "We interpret the statement as no more than a recognition by the court that the substantiality of the question to be at issue on the merits of the appeal was a matter appropriate for its consideration under Rule 73(a), in connection with all the other circumstances before it. Rule 73(a) is intended to place reliance upon the sound discretion of the Circuit Court of Appeals. We see no reason to question the discretion exercised in this case as evidenced by the agreement of all of the five Circuit Judges to whom the issue was presented. Ainsworth v. Gill Glass & Fixture Co. 3 Cir, 104 F2d 

83; Mutual Benefit Health & Accident Ass'n v. Snyder, 6 Cir, 109 F2d 469; 

Burke v. Canfield, 72 App DC 127, 111 F2d 526; United States v. Gallagher, 9 Cir, 151 F2d 556. "Accordingly, we sustain the denial of the motion to dismiss the appeal under Rule 73(a).”

Subdivisions (a), (b), (d), (g), and (h) were amended; new subdivisions (m), (n), and (o) were added; subdivisions (c), (e), (f), (i), (j), (k), and (1) remain unchanged. 175.01, infra.

The basic principles involved in preparing a record on appeal to a circuit court of appeals under Rule 75 have not changed. This is shown in part from an examination of the subdivisions that remain unchanged. The 1946 revision made no changes in the following matters:

Subdivision (c), form of testimony-testimony may be either in narrative or in question and answer form;

Subdivision (e), record to be abbreviated; 

Subdivision (f), parties may stipulate as to contents of the record; 

Subdivision (i), district court may cause original papers or exhibits to be sent to the circuit court of appeals; 

Subdivision (j), dealing with the record for a preliminary hearing in the appellate court;

Subdivision (k), providing for a single record on appeal when more than one appeal is taken to the same court from the same judgment; and 

Subdivision (1), subjecting the printing of the record on appeal to the rules of the particular circuit court of appeals, except that "the type, paper, and dimensions of printed matter in the circuit court of appeals shall conform to the Rules of the Supreme Court relating to records on appeals to that court."

Of the three new subdivisions that were added, only one is of general importance. Subdivisions (m) and (n), which deal, respectively, with the record in an in forma pauperis appeal, and when no stenographic report was made, will have applicability only in the marginal cases. New subdivision (o), on the other hand, has potentialities of considerable importance. It permits and regulates the practice for the transmission of the original papers, whenever a circuit court of appeals provides by rule for hearing of appeals on the original papers. While containing possibilities, the effectiveness of this subdivision must depend upon complementary rules of the various circuit courts of appeals.

And as we shall presently see, the amendments to subdivisions (a), (b), (d), (g), and (h) are largely amplifying. Perhaps the amendment to subdivision (b), which, in general, requires only one copy of the reporter's transcript to be filed, in lieu of the former requirement of two copies, is the most important.

Subdivision (a). This subdivision was amended in the following respects (old matter stricken out is in brackets, new matter is in italics): 

"(a) Designation of Contents of Record on Appeal. Promptly after an appeal to a circuit court of appeals is taken, the appellant shall serve upon

¶ 0.01 

the appellee and file with the district court a designation of the portions of the record, proceedings, and evidence to be contained in the record on appeal, unless the appellee has already served and filed a designation. Within 10 days [thereafter] after the service and filing of such a designation, any other party to the appeal may serve and file a designation of additional portions of the record, proceedings, and evidence to be included. If the appellee files the original designation, the parties shall proceed under subdivision (b) of this rule as if the appellee were the appellant.” 

While a party desiring to appeal must still take the first step of filing the notice of appeal pursuant to Rule 73 (a), amended Rule 75(a) permits an appellee to take the initiative in preparing the record for transmission to the appellate court if he desires to expedite the hearing of the appeal. The rights of an appellant to a record adequate to present his case are fully protected, since he is accorded all the rights the appellee would normally have had to designate material to be included. In most cases an appellee will not find it sufficiently advantageous to him to take the initiative in preparing the record on appeal, and hence the amendatory provisions will probably be rarely invoked.

Although subdivision (a) requires a designation of the portions of the record, proceedings, and evidence to be contained in the record on appeal, to be served and filed "promptly" after an appeal is taken, what constitutes promptness must depend largely upon the nature of the matters which the appellant seeks to have reviewed. Thus where a review is sought solely on questions of pleading, the designation can, as a practical matter, be prepared, served and filed contemporaneously with, or shortly after, the perfecting of the appeal. But where the appellant desires to raise questions that can be determined only by reference to testimony, the time which will constitute prompt service and filing of the designation is a variable time. 

Suppose that after an extended hearing or trial the judgment is entered and the losing party effects an appeal within a few days. As a physical matter it may be days and even weeks before the appellant can secure a copy of the reporter's transcript, and yet he is obliged by Rule 75(b) to file with his designation a copy of the reporter's transcript, unless it is already on file. 

The same problem will confront the appellee if he takes advantage of amended Rule 75(a) and assumes the initiative in the preparation of the record on appeal. While the question of promptness must depend upon the type of record that is necessary for the review sought, where the appellant designates for inclusion something less than the complete record and all the proceedings and evidence in the action he must not so delay the service of his designation as to deprive the appellee of the 10-day period within which to serve and file an additional designation under subdivision (a). 

Subdivision (b). This subdivision was amended in the following respects (old matter stricken out is in brackets, new matter is in italics): "(b) Transcript. If there be designated for inclusion any evidence or proceedings at a trial or hearing which was stenographically reported, the appellant shall file with his designation [two copies] a copy of the reporter's transcript of the evidence or proceedings included in his designation. If the designation includes only part of the reporter's transcript, the appellant shall file [two copies] a copy of such additional parts thereof as the appellee

¶ 0.152 

may need to enable him to designate and file the parts he desires to have added, and if the appellant fails to do so the court on motion may require him to furnish the additional parts needed. [One of the copies] The copy so filed by the appellant shall be available for the use of the other parties [and for use in the appellate court in printing the record]. In the event that a copy of the reporter's transcript or of the necessary portions thereof is already on file, the appellant shall not be required to file an additional copy. 

When the rules of the circuit court of appeals so require, the appellant shall furnish a second copy of the transcript for use in the appellate court." 

Changes here made are certainly in the interest of economy. Whereas the original subdivision required the appellant to file two copies of the reporter's transcript, the amended subdivision requires the appellant to file only one copy, unless the rules of the circuit court of appeals require a second copy of the transcript for use in the appellate court, in which latter event the cost of a second copy must be incurred. Since a majority of the clerks of the circuit courts of appeals stated that the second copy required by original subdivision (b) was unnecessary, it is fair to assume that most of the circuit courts of appeals will not require a second copy. Furthermore, under the amended subdivision if a copy of the reporter's transcript or the necessary portions thereof is already on file this will suffice as one copy. Thus unless the rules of the circuit court of appeals require a second copy, the appellant need not file any copy of the reporter's transcript if one is already on file; and if a circuit court of appeals requires a second copy he would in that event be under the necessity of furnishing only one copy himself. In some cases a copy of the reporter's transcript will be on file by virtue of the Official Court Reporter Act, 28 USC § 9a. Under subdivision (b) of this section it is provided: "Upon the request of any party to any proceeding which has been so recorded and who has agreed to pay the fee therefor or of a judge of the court, the reporter shall promptly transcribe the original records of the requested parts of the proceedings and attach thereto his official certificate, and shall then deliver the transcript to the party or judge making the request. . . The reporter shall promptly deliver to the clerk for the records of the court a certified copy of any transcript that he may make of any part of the record of any proceeding." Thus there will already be on file a copy of the reporter's transcript, as referred to in Rule 75(b), in at least the following situations: (1) when the judge has requested the official court reporter to make up a transcript for his (the judge's) use; or (2) when either one or both of the parties request and obtain a day-to-day transcript of the trial.

Subdivision (d). The 1946 amendments inserted at the beginning of this subdivision what is now the first sentence. It reads: "No assignment of errors is necessary." 22 This is only a clear statement of the correct practice under the subdivision as originally promulgated.

Subdivision (g). The first two sentences of this subdivision provide that the clerk is to certify the record on appeal and transmit it to the appellate court; and prescribe the minimum matter which that record must contain. 

No changes were made in these two sentences. What was the last sentence

¶ 0.01 

was amended and a new sentence was added (old matter stricken out is in brackets, new matter is in italics): "The clerk shall transmit with the record on appeal a copy thereof [for use in printing the record, if] when a copy is required by the rules of the circuit court of appeals. The copy of the transcript filed as provided in subdivision (b) of this rule shall be certified by the clerk as a part of the record on appeal and the clerk may not require an additional copy as a requisite to certification."

These changes are complementary to the changes made in subdivision (b). 

Thus there need be no second copy of the record on appeal unless required by the rules of the circuit court of appeals. And no additional copy of the transcript filed as provided in Rule 75(b) may be required. In other words, one copy of the transcript is sufficient for all purposes, unless an additional copy is expressly required by the rules of the circuit court of appeals. 

Subdivision (h). This subdivision was amended in the following respects (new matter is in italics): "(h) Power of Court to Correct or Modify Record. It is not necessary for the record on appeal to be approved by the district court or judge thereof, except as provided in subdivisions (m) and (n) of this rule and in Rule 76, but, if any difference arises as to whether the record truly discloses what occurred in the district court, the difference shall be submitted to and settled by that court and the record made to conform to the truth. If anything material to either party is omitted from the record on appeal by error or accident or is misstated therein, the parties by stipulation, or the district court, either before or after the record is transmitted to the appellate court, or the appellate court, on a proper suggestion or of its own initiative, may direct that the omission or missstatement shall be corrected, and if necessary that a supplemental record shall be certified and transmitted by the clerk of the district court. All other questions as to the content and form of the record shall be presented to the circuit court of appeals." 

The except clause introduced into the first sentence effects no substantial change in the former practice which, in the main, did not require court approval of a record prepared in accordance with Rule 75. This except clause requires court approval of a record as provided in new subdivision (m) dealing with appeals in forma pauperis, new subdivision (n) dealing with appeals when no stenographic report was made, and Rule 76 dealing with the agreed statement on appeal. Rule 76 always required the agreed statement to be approved by the district court and an omission of a reference to Rule 76 under original subdivision (h) was inadvertent. Cases involving an appeal when no stenographic report was made and an appeal in forma pauperis are few in number as compared to the great bulk of appeals. Furthermore, because of the Official Court Reporter Act the reporter's transcript will now be available, as a general proposition, to the pauper appellant, and subdivision (m) will not have great applicability even in pauper appeals (see Comment under subdivision (m), infra). It can, therefore, be seen that the exceptions introduced into subdivision (h) are very limited. The general proposition is, and this is fortified by the new sentence which was added at the end of the rule, that the district court need not approve the record on appeal. The Committee had before it and definitely rejected the suggestion

"that the procedure now provided for in these rules allowing parties to designate what is to be included in the record on appeals should be abolished, and that the rules should provide that the record shall be made up under the supervision and with the approval of the district judge." This suggestion was rejected for the following reasons. "The present system providing for designation by the parties of what is to be included in the record on appeal has been the system prescribed by the equity rules for many years and has generally worked to the satisfaction of the bench and bar." A busy district judge will normally not comb through a record on appeal that is satisfactory to the parties for the purpose of eliminating superfluous matter; and the district judges generally do not favor the opportunity or duty to supervise the preparation of the record. "Furthermore, it is thought that a lawyer has the right to have incorporated in the record anything which actually occurred in the trial court which he thinks necessary to make his points on appeal. There have been occasions-fortunately rare-where a district judge has refused, in settling a case or bill of exceptions, to include matters which actually occurred in the trial court. The general trend is all against limiting by action of the district court the material to go into the record on appeal." Committee Note 2 of 1946 to Rule 75.

Subdivision (m). This subdivision which deals with appeals in forma pauperis is new, and gives the district court power to "specify some different and more economical manner by which the record on appeal may be prepared and settled, to the end that the appellant may be enabled to present his case to the appellate court." 

In the past, difficulty has been encountered by a pauper appellant in obtaining the reporter's transcript of the evidence. While 28 USC § 832 allowed a citizen of the United States under certain circumstances to proceed in forma pauperis, without the prepayment of fees or costs, to institute or defend an action and take an appeal "unless the trial court shall certify in writing that in the opinion of the court such appeal . . . is not taken in good faith" it was generally ruled that the charges of a court reporter are not in their inception "fees or costs" within the meaning of this section, and hence a pauper appellant could not obtain the reporter's transcript without the prepayment of his fees. Estabrook v. King (CCA8th, 1941) 119 F2d 607;

Cheek v. Thompson (WD La 1940) 33 F Supp 497. By virtue of subdivision 

(m) a district court may now specify a record on appeal somewhat along the lines where no stenographic report was made, as provided in subdivision (n), and thus assist a pauper appellant. But because of the Official Court 

Reporter Act of 1944, the court will seldom need to invoke the power given it by subdivision (m). The Reporter Act provides: "Fees for transcripts furnished in criminal or habeas corpus proceedings to persons allowed to sue, defend, or appeal in forma pauperis, shall be paid by the United States out of money appropriated for that purpose; and the fees for transcripts furnished in other than criminal or habeas corpus proceedings to persons permitted to appeal in forma pauperis shall also be paid by the United States. if the trial judge or a circuit judge shall certify that the appeal is not frivolous but presents a substantial question. Except as to transcripts that are to be paid for by the United States, the reporter may require any party re-

¶ 0.01 

1 3 questing a transcript to prepay the estimated fee therefor in advance of delivery of the transcript." 28 USC § 9a (c).

Subdivision (n). This subdivision (n) is new and was added to care for the rare case when no stenographic report of the evidence was made. In view of the Official Court Reporter Act, 28 USC § 9a, it is seldom that a stenographic report will not be made. The provisions of subdivision (n) may, nevertheless, be helpful as supplemental safeguards to prevent injustice in unusual cases; and there is nothing in the subdivision that is inconsistent with the Act.

Subdivision (o). This subdivision is new. The Committee in its 1946 Note states the reasons therefor: "The addition of subdivision (o) to Rule 75 is in response to the suggestion of circuit judges that such a provision would be helpful to those circuit courts of appeals which desire to permit the transmittal of the original papers in the proceeding rather than a copy or a 'transcript' of the papers. 

This course avoids the delay and expense entailed by the preparation of copies and gives the appellate court the advantage of having before it the original papers just as they were presented to the trial court. It appears particularly appropriate for those appellate courts which by rule have dispensed with formal printing of the record. The provision in the subdivision dealing with the time in which the clerk shall act gives the parties an opportunity to inspect papers while preparing their briefs." 

1 Rule 76. Record on Appeal to a Circuit Court of Ap- 

2 peals; Agreed Statement. When the questions presented 

3 by an appeal to a circuit court of appeals can be determined 4 without an examination of all the pleadings, evidence, and 5 proceedings in the court below, the parties may prepare 6 and sign a statement of the case showing how the questions 7 arose and were decided in the district court and setting 8 forth only so many of the facts averred and proved or 9 sought to be proved as are essential to a decision of the 10 questions by the appellate court. The statement shall in- 

11 clude a copy of the judgment appealed from, a copy of 12 the notice of appeal with its filing date, and a concise state- 13 ment of the points to be relied on by the appellant. If 

14 the statement conforms to the truth, it, together with such 15 additions as the court may consider necessary fully to 16 present the questions raised by the appeal, shall be ap- 17 proved by the district court and shall then be certified to 18 the appellate court as the record on appeal.

 X. DISTRICT COURTS AND CLERKS 

1 Rule 77. District Courts and Clerks. 2 

(a) DISTRICT COURTS ALWAYS OPEN. The district courts 

3 shall be deemed always open for the purpose of filing any 4 pleading or other proper paper, of issuing and returning 5 mesne and final process, and of making and directing all in- 

6 terlocutory motions, orders, and rules. 

7 (b) TRIALS AND HEARINGS; ORDERS IN CHAMBERS. All 

8 trials upon the merits shall be conducted in open court 9 and so far as convenient in a regular court room. All 

10 other acts or proceedings may be done or conducted by a 11 judge in chambers, without the attendance of the clerk 12 or other court officials and at any place either within or 13 without the district; but no hearing, other than one ex 

14 parte, shall be conducted outside the district without the 15 consent of all parties affected thereby. 

16 (c) CLERK'S OFFICE AND ORDERS BY CLERK. The clerk's 

17 office with the clerk or a deputy in attendance shall be open 18 during business hours on all days except Sundays and 

19 legal holidays. All motions and applications in the clerk's 

20 office for issuing mesne process, for issuing final process to 21 enforce and execute judgments, for entering defaults or 22 judgments by default, and for other proceedings which 23 do not require allowance or order of the court are grant- 

24 able of course by the clerk; but his action may be suspend- 

25 ed or altered or rescinded by the court upon cause shown. 

26 (d) NOTICE OF ORDERS OR JUDGMENTS. Immediately upon 

27 the entry of an order or judgment the clerk shall serve a 28 notice of the entry by mail in the manner provided for 29 in Rule 5 upon every party affected thereby who is not in 30 default for failure to appear, and shall make a note in 31 the docket of the mailing. Such mailing is sufficient notice 

32 for all purposes for which notice of the entry of an order is 33 required by these rules; but any party may in addition 

34 serve a notice of such entry in the manner provided in Rule 35 5 for the service of papers. Lack of notice of the entry by 

36 the clerk does not affect the time to appeal or relieve or au- 37 thorize the court to relieve a party for failure to appeal 38 within the time allowed, except as permitted in Rule 73(a).

Comment

Subdivision (d) was amended; subdivisions (a), (b), and (c) remain unchanged. 77.01, infra.

Subdivision (d). This subdivision was amended by adding what is now the concluding sentence, and which reads: "Lack of notice of the entry by the clerk does not affect the time to appeal or relieve or authorize the court to relieve a party for failure to appeal within the time allowed, except as permitted in Rule 73(a)." 

The 1946 revision buttressed the finality of judgments by amending Rule 6(b) so that the court could not extend the time periods stated in certain rules, most of which bear upon the finality of a judgment; by amending 

Rule 6(c) so that the "continued existence" of a term of court gives the district court no additional power; by amending Rule 59 (b) so that a motion for a new trial on any ground must be served not later than 10 days after the entry of the judgment; by amending Rule 60 (b) dealing with relief from a judgment; and by amending Rule 73 (a) to reduce, in general, the time for appeal, and to state the effect of certain motions, provided they are timely, upon the running of the time for appeal. See Comments under these rules, supra. The amendment to subdivision (d) of Rule 77 is in line with the general philosophy in support of the finality of a judgment. 

As previously stated the time for appeal commences to run from the entry of a judgment as provided in Rule 58, and no notice is necessary to start the appeal time running. See Comments under Rules 5, 6, 58, 72, and 73, supra.

The amendment to Rule 77 (d) clearly recognizes this principle, although it recognizes that lack of notice of the entry of a judgment should give the court power to extend the time for appeal to a circuit court of appeals as provided in Rule 73(a). The amendment does not, however, recognize a power in the district court to extend the time for a direct appeal to the Supreme Court because of lack of notice of the entry of the judgment. 

Comment under Rule 72, supra.

See The reasons behind the amendment of Rule 77 (d) are stated by the Committee in its 1946 Note: "Rule 77 (d) has been amended to avoid such situations as the one arising in Hill v. Hawes (1944) 320 US 520. In that case, an action instituted in the District Court for the District of Columbia, the clerk failed to give notice of the entry of a judgment for defendant as required by Rule 77 (d). The time for taking an appeal then was 20 days under Rule 10 of the Court of Appeals (later enlarged by amendment to thirty days), and due to lack of notice of the entry of judgment the plaintiff failed to file his notice of appeal within the prescribed time. On this basis the trial court vacated the original judgment and then re-entered it, whereupon notice of appeal was filed. The 

Court of Appeals dismissed the appeal as taken too late. The Supreme Court, however, held that although Rule 77 (d) did not purport to attach any consequence to the clerk's failure to give notice as specified, the terms of the rule were such that the appellant was entitled to rely on it, and the trial court in such a case, in the exercise of a sound discretion, could vacate the former judgment and enter a new one, so that the appeal would be within the allowed time.

¶ 0.01 

"Because of Rule 6(c), which abolished the old rule that the expiration of the term ends a court's power over its judgment, the effect of the decision in Hill v. Hawes is to give the district court power, in its discretion and without time limit, and long after the term may have expired, to vacate a judgment and re-enter it for the purpose of reviving the right of appeal. This seriously affects the finality of judgments. See also proposed Rule 6(c) and Note; proposed Rule 60 (b) and Note; and Proposed Rule 73 (a) and Note. [The proposed amendments to these rules were adopted and promulgated by the Court in its 1946 revision.] "Rule 77(d) as amended makes it clear that notificaton by the clerk of the entry of a judgment has nothing to do with the starting of the time for appeal; that time starts to run from the date of entry of judgment and not from the date of notice of the entry. Notification by the clerk is merely for the convenience of litigants. And lack of such notification in itself has no effect upon the time for appeal; but in considering an application for extension of time for appeal as provided in Rule 73(a), the court may take into account, as one of the factors affecting its decision, whether the clerk failed to give notice as provided in Rule 77 (d) or the party failed to receive the clerk's notice. It need not, however, extend the time for appeal merely because the clerk's notice was not sent or received. It would, therefore, be entirely unsafe for a party to rely on absence of notice from the clerk of the entry of a judgment, or to rely on the adverse party's failure to serve notice of the entry of a judgment. Any party may, of course, serve timely notice of the entry of a judgment upon the adverse party and thus preclude a successful application, under Rule 73(a), for the extension of the time for appeal." [See Comment under Rule 5, supra.]

Comment 

The title of Rule 79 was enlarged to include a reference to records (the enlargement is shown in italics): "Books and Records Kept by the Clerk and Entries Therein." All subdivisions, (a), (b), and (c), were amended; and a new subdivision (d) was added. ¶79.01, infra.

Subdivision (a). Only the first sentence of this subdivision was amended, and in the following respects (old matter stricken out is in brackets, new matter is in italics): "The clerk shall keep a book known as 'civil docket' of such form and style as may be prescribed by the [Attorney General under the authority of the 

Act of June 30, 1906, c 3914, § 1 (34 Stat 754), as amended, USC, Title 28, § 568, or other statutory authority,] Director of the Administrative Office of the United States Courts with the approval of the Judicial Conference of Senior Circuit Judges, and shall enter therein each civil action to which these rules are made applicable."

The Committee in its 1946 Note states the function of the revision. "The amendment substitutes the Director of the Administrative Office of the United 

States Courts, acting subject to the approval of the Judicial Conference of Senior Circuit Judges, in the place of the Attorney General as a consequence of and in accordance with the provisions of the act establishing the Administrative Office and transferring functions thereto. Act of August 7, 1939, c 

501, §§ 1-7, 53 Stat 1223, 28 USC §§ 444-450." 

Entry of judgment. The notation of a judgment in the civil docket as provided in subdivision (a) of Rule 79 constitutes the entry of the judgment pursuant to Rule 58, and the time for appeal commences to run therefrom. See Comments under Rules 5, 58, 72, 73, and 77, supra.

Subdivision (b). This subdivision was amended in the following respects (old matter stricken out is in brackets, new matter is in italics): 

"(b) Civil [Order Book] Judgments and Orders. The clerk shall [also] 

keep [a book for civil actions entitled 'civil order book' in which shall be kept in the sequence of their making exact copies of all final judgments and orders, all orders,] in such form and manner as the Director of the Administrative Office of the United States Courts with the approval of the Judicial Conference of Senior Circuit Judges may prescribe, a correct copy of every final judgment or appealable order, or order affecting title to or lien upon real or personal property, [all appealable orders,] and [such] any other order [s as] which the court may direct to be kept." 

The Committee in its Note of 1946 states: "The change in this subdivision does not alter the nature of the judgments and orders to be recorded in permanent form but it does away with the express requirement that they be recorded in a book. This merely gives latitude for the preservation of court records in other than book form, if that shall seem advisable, and permits with the approval of the Judicial Conference the adoption of such modern, VOL. 1-MOORE F.P.

space-saving methods as microphotography. See Proposed Improvements in the Administration of the Offices of Clerks of United States District Courts, prepared by the Bureau of the Budget (1941) 38-42. See also Rule 55, Federal Rules of Criminal Procedure."

Subdivision (c). This subdivision was amended in the following respects (old matter stricken out is in brackets, new matter is in italics): "(e) Indices; Calendars. [Separate and] Suitable indices of the civil docket and of [the] every civil [order book] judgment and order referred to in subdivision (b) of this rule shall be kept by the clerk under the direction of the court. There shall be prepared under the direction of the court calendars of all actions ready for trial, which shall distinguish 'jury actions' from 'court actions"."

The Committee in its Note of 1946 states: "The words 'Separate and' have been deleted as unduly rigid. There is no sufficient reason for requiring that the indices in all cases be separate; on the contrary, the requirement frequently increases the labor of persons searching the records as well as the labor of the clerk's force preparing them. The matter should be left to administrative discretion. "The other changes in the subdivision merely conform with those made in subdivision (b) of the rule."

Subdivision (d). This subdivision was added by the 1946 amendments. 

It enables the Administrative Office, with the approval of the Judicial Conference of Senior Circuit Judges, to carry out any improvements in clerical procedure with respect to books and records which may be deemed advisable. 1 

Rule 80. Stenographer; Stenographic Report or Tran- 

2 script as Evidence. 12345 3

Subdivision (c) remains unchanged; subdivisions (a) and (b), as originally promulgated, were abrogated because of the Official Court Reporter 

Act of 1944, 28 USC § 9(a). See [80.01, infra.

The Court Reporter Act provides for the appointment of official stenographers for each district court, prescribes their duties, provides for the furnishing of transcripts, the taxation of the fees therefor as costs, and other related matters. For discussion of its bearing upon the preparation

¶ 0.01 

of a record on appeal to a circuit court of appeals, see Comments under 

Rule 75(b), (m), and (n), supra.

Subdivisions (a) and (b), as originally promulgated, provided: "(a) Stenographer. A court or master may direct that evidence be taken stenographically and may appoint a stenographer for that purpose. His fees shall be fixed by the court and may be taxed ultimately as costs, in the discretion of the court. The cost of a transcript shall be paid in the first instance by the party ordering the transcript. "(b) Official Stenographers. Each district court may designate one or more official court stenographers for the district and fix by rule of court the compensation which such stenographers shall be entitled to charge for their services, with provision that amounts properly paid by parties for the service of such stenographers be taxable as costs in the case in the discretion of the trial judge. The work of the stenographers shall be so arranged as to avoid delay in furnishing transcripts ordered for the purposes of motions for new trial, for amended findings, or for appeals."

XI. GENERAL PROVISIONS 

Rule 81. Applicability in General. 

(a) TO WHAT PROCEEDINGS APPLICABLE. 

(1) These rules do not apply to proceedings in admiralty. They do not apply to proceedings in bankruptcy or proceedings in copyright under the Act of 

March 4, 1909, c 320, § 25 (35 Stat 1081), as amended,

USC, Title 17, § 25, except in so far as they may be made applicable thereto by rules promulgated by the Supreme Court of the United States. They do not apply to probate, adoption, or lunacy proceedings in the

District Court of the United States for the District of 

Columbia except to appeals therein. (2) In the following proceedings appeals are governed by these rules, but they are not applicable otherwise than on appeal except to the extent that the practice in such proceedings is not set forth in statutes of the United States and has heretofore conformed to the practice in actions at law or suits in equity: admission to citizenship, habeas corpus, quo warranto, and forfeiture of property for violation of a statute of the 

United States. The requirements of USC, Title 28, 

§ 466, relating to certification of probable cause in certain appeals in habeas corpus cases remain in force. (3) In proceedings under the Act of February 12, 1925, c 213 (43 Stat 883), USC, Title 9, relating to arbitration, or under the Act of May 20, 1926, c 

347, § 9 (44 Stat 585), USC, Title 45, § 159, relat ing to boards of arbitration of railway labor disputes, these rules apply to appeals, but otherwise only to the extent that matters of procedure are not provided for in those statutes. These rules apply (1) to proceedings to compel the giving of testimony or production of documents in accordance with a subpoena issued by an officer or agency of the United States under any statute of the United States except as otherwise provided by statute or by rules of the district court or by order of the court in the proceedings, and (2) to appeals in such proceedings.

388), USC, Title 7, § 292; or by the Act of June 10, 1930, c 436, § 7 (46 Stat 534), as amended, USC, Title 7, § 499g (c), for instituting proceedings in the district courts of the United States to review orders of the Secretary of Agriculture; or prescribed by the Act of June 25, 1934, c 742, § 2 (48 Stat 1214), USC, Title 15, § 522, for instituting proceedings to review orders of the Secretary of Commerce; or prescribed by the Act of February 22, 1935, c 18, 

§ 5 (49 Stat 31), USC, Title 15, § 715d (c), as extended, for instituting proceedings to review orders of petroleum control boards; but the conduct of such proceedings in the district courts shall be made to conform to these rules so far as applicable. Subdivisions (a) and (c) were amended; a new subdivision (f) was added; subdivisions (b), (d), and (e) remain unchanged. 181.01, infra.

In 1939 the Supreme Court amended Rule 81(a)(6) to make the Rule generally applicable to proceedings for enforcement or review of compensation orders under the Longshoremen's and Harbor Workers' Compensation Act. 

See under subdivision (a), paragraph (6), infra. The effective date of this 

1939 amendment was April 3, 1941. See Comment under Rule 86, infra.

The 1946 amendments made no substantial changes as to the applicability of the Rules. The most important change in Rule 81 was the addition of subdivision (f) for the purpose of defining clearly that the term "officer" of the United States "includes a collector of internal revenue, a former collector of internal revenue, or the personal representative of a deceased collector of internal revenue". Probably the next amendment in importance is to subdivision (c) providing that in removed actions the maximum time allowed to the removing party within which to answer or present other defenses or objections is 20 days after the filing of the transcript. 

Subdivision (a). Paragraph (1) remains unchanged. The Rules apply to civil actions by and against the United States, but do not extend jurisdiction. 

While the Federal Rules do not by their own force apply to proceedings in bankruptcy and copyright, they have, however, been made generally applicable to those proceedings; certain of the Federal Rules have also been adopted in admiralty; and the Criminal Rules were patterned to a great extent upon the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure. See Comment under 

Rule 1, supra.

Paragraph (2). This paragraph was amended by adding what is now the concluding sentence, and which reads: "The requirements of USC, Title 28, $ 466, relating to certification of probable cause in certain appeals in habeas corpus cases remain in force." 

Under this statute there is no appeal to the circuit court of appeals where the detention complained of is by virtue of process issued out of the state court, "unless the United States court by which the final decision was rendered or

¶ 0.168 

a judge of the circuit court of appeals shall be of the opinion that there exists probable cause for an appeal, in which event, on allowing the same, the said court or judge shall certify that there is probable cause for such allowance". 

The amendment to paragraph (2) of Rule 81 (a) merely makes clear that the Rules have not superseded this statutory requirement, and merely adopts what had been held, under the Rules as originally promulgated, in Schenk v. Plummer (CCA9th, 1940) 113 F2d 726 (second case).

Forfeiture proceedings.-No change was made in paragraph (2) relative to forfeiture proceedings, but the Committee in its Note of 1946 stated: "For correct application of the rules in proceedings for forfeiture of property for violation of a statute of the United States, such as under USC, Title 22, § 405 (seizure of war materials intended for unlawful export) or USC, Title 

21, § 334(b) (Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act; formerly Title 21, § 14, 

Pure Food and Drug Act), see Reynal v. United States (CCA5th, 1945) 153 

F2d 929; United States v. 108 Boxes of Cheddar Cheese (SD Iowa 1943) 3 

FRD 40." In the Reynal case, supra, Judge Hutcheson stated: "We agree with the government that, except as to filing the libel and obtaining jurisdiction, admiralty procedure does not apply. A forfeiture proceeding after these preliminaries takes the character of a law action, and under Rule 81(a) (2), Federal Rules of Civil Procedure, is now governed by those rules. Therefore, appellant may not invoke Admiralty Rule 39 for setting aside a default.

The motion to set the judgment aside was a motion to set aside an entry of default. It was governed by Rule 55(c) and 60(b), Rules of 

Civil Procedure." In the Cheddar Cheese case, supra, Judge Dewey denied the intervening claimant's motion for a more specific statement on the ground that claimant could obtain the desired information by invoking Federal Rule 33, dealing with interrogatories to parties. For the effect to be given a Committee Note construing a rule, see [1.13 [2], infra.

Paragraph (3). This paragraph was amended by adding what is now the concluding sentence and which reads: "These rules apply (1) to proceedings to compel the giving of testimony or production of documents in accordance with a subpoena issued by an officer or agency of the United States under any statute of the United States except as otherwise provided by statute or by rules of the district court or by order of the court in the proceedings, and (2) to appeals in such proceedings."

This is largely a clarifying amendment which (1) makes the Rules generally applicable to proceedings for the enforcement of administrative subpoenas in line with case law, and (2) clearly makes appeals in such proceedings subject to the Federal Rules. 

Paragraph (4). This paragraph remains unchanged. 

Paragraph (5). This paragraph remains unchanged.

Paragraph (6). As originally promulgated this paragraph provided, inter alia, that the Rules did not apply to proceedings for review of compensation orders under the Longshoremen's and Harbor Workers' Compensation Act. Then by order of December 28, 1939, the Supreme Court amended paragraph (6) to make the Rules "apply to proceedings for enforcement or review of compensation orders under the Longshoremen's and Harbor Work-

¶ 0.01 

 ers' Compensation Act, Act of March 4, 1927, c 509, §§ 18, 21 (44 Stat 1434, 1436), USC, Title 33, §§ 918, 921, except to the extent that matters of procedure are provided for in that Act." This amendment became effective 

April 3, 1941. See Comment under Rule 86, infra.

Then by the 1946 amendments the court amended the concluding sentence in the following minor respects (old matter stricken out is in brackets, new matter is in italics): "The provisions for service by publication and [allowing the defendant 60 days within which to] for answer in proceedings to cancel certificates of citizenship under the Act of June 29, 1906, e 3592, § 15 (34 Stat 601), as amended, USC, Title 8, §[405,] 738, remain in effect." 

The reason for this change is stated by the Committee in its 1946 Note as follows: "Section 405 of USC, Title 8 originally referred to in the last sentence of paragraph (6), has been repealed and § 738, USC, Title 8, has been enacted in its stead. The last sentence of paragraph (6) has, therefore, been amended in accordance with this change. The sentence has also been amended so as to refer directly to the statute regarding the provision of time for answer, thus avoiding any confusion attendant upon a change in the statute."

Paragraph (7). This paragraph which makes the Rules inapplicable to condemnation proceedings, except as to appeals, remains unchanged. The 

Advisory Committee has, however, under consideration a proposed Rule 71A, which would provide a uniform procedure for federal condemnation cases. 

See its Preliminary Draft of June, 1947. The Committee has not as of 1947 recommended any condemnation rule to the Court. 

Subdivision (c). The third sentence of this subdivision was amended to read (new matter is shown in italics): "In a removed action in which the defendant has not answered, he shall answer or present the other defenses or objections available to him under these rules within the time allowed for answer by the law of the state or within 5 days after the filing of the transcript of the record in the district court of the United States, whichever period is longer, but in any event within 20 days after the filing of the transcript."

The reason for this is stated in the Committee Note of 1946: "The change in subdivision (c) effects more speedy trials in removed actions. In some states many of the courts have only two terms a year. A case, if filed 20 days before a term, is returnable to that term, but if filed less than 20 days before a term, is returnable to the following term, which convenes six months later. Hence, under the original wording of Rule 81(c), where a case is filed less than 20 days before the term and is removed within a few days but before answer, it is possible for the defendant to delay interposing his answer or presenting his defenses by motion for six months or more. The rule as amended prevents this result."

Subdivision (f). This subdivision was added by the 1946 amendments. "The use of the phrase 'the United States or an officer or agency thereof' in the rules (as e. g., in Rule 12(a) and amended Rule 73(a)) could raise the question of whether 'officer' includes a collector of internal revenue, a former collector, or the personal representative of a deceased collector, against whom

0.170 

suits for tax refunds are frequently instituted. Difficulty might ensue for the reason that a suit against a collector or his representative has been held to be a personal action. Sage v. United States (1919) 250 US 33; Smietanka v. 

Indiana Steel Co. (1921) 257 US 1; United States v. Nunnally Investment 

Co. (1942) 316 US 258. The addition of subdivision (f) to Rule 81 dispels any doubts on the matter and avoids further litigation." Committee Note of 1946.

Comment 

Rule 85 remains unchanged. 985.01, infra. 1 

Rule 86. Effective Date. These rules will take effect 

2 on the day which is 3 months subsequent to the adjourn- 3 ment of the second regular session of the 75th Congress, 4 but if that day is prior to September 1, 1938, 5 then these rules will take effect on September 1, 1938. 

6 They govern all proceedings in actions brought after they 7 take effect and also all further proceedings in actions then 8 pending, except to the extent that in the opinion of the 9 court their application in a particular action pending when 10 the rules take effect would not be feasible or would work 11 injustice, in which event the former procedure applies. 

12 (b) EFFECTIVE DATE OF AMENDMENTS. The amendments 

13 adopted by the Supreme Court on December 27, 1946, and 

14 transmitted to the Attorney General on January 2, 1947, 15 shall take effect on the day which is three months subse- 16 quent to the adjournment of the first regular session of 

17 the 80th Congress, but, if that day is prior to September 1, 18 1947, then these amendments shall take effect on Septem- 

19 ber 1, 1947. They govern all proceedings in actions 

20 brought after they take effect and also all further proceed- 21 ings in actions then pending, except to the extent that in 22 the opinion of the court their application in a particular 23 action pending when the amendments take effect would 24 not be feasible or would work injustice, in which event the 

25 former procedure applies.

¶ 0.172 

Comment

The first paragraph, which was Rule 86 as originally promulgated, stated how the effective date of the original Federal Rules should be determined. 

The original Federal Rules were promulgated by the Court on December 20, 1937, transmitted by the Attorney General to Congress on January 3, 1938, and became effective September 16, 1938.

Since promulgation the Rules have been amended twice: once in 1939 as to a minor detail; and then extensively revised in 1946. 

The 1939 amendment. By order of December 28, 1939 the Supreme Court amended Rule 81 (a) (6) to make the Rules generally applicable to proceedings for enforcement or review of compensation orders under the Longshoremen's and Harbor Workers' Compensation Act. See Comment under Rule 

81(a)(6), supra. As a part of that order it was provided that the amendment shall "take effect on the day which is three months subsequent to the adjournment of the second regular session of the 76th Congress, but if that day is prior to September 1, 1940, then this amendment shall take effect on 

September 1, 1940." And the order also provided that the Chief Justice be authorized to transmit this amendment to the Attorney General with the request that he report it to Congress at the beginning of the regular session in January, 1940. Thus the same formal procedure in transmitting this minor amendment to Congress and in delaying its effective date was followed as in the original promulgation of the Rules. And since the second session of the 

76th Congress did not adjourn until January 3, 1941, the simple amendment to Rule 81(a)(6) did not become effective until April 3, 1941. 

The 1946 amendments. On December 27, 1946 the Supreme Court promul gated the other amendments which have been heretofore discussed. As a part of the 1946 amendments it added what is now subdivision (b) to Rule 86 to deal with the effective date of the 1946 amendments. The same procedure was followed in relation to the 1946 amendments as was followed in the original promulgation of the Rules and in amending Rule 81 (a) (6) in 1939, to wit, the Chief Justice transmitted the 1946 amendments to the Attorney General with the request that he report them to Congress at the beginning of the regular session of the 80th Congress in January, 1947. The amendments were so transmitted and reported. The first regular session of the 

80th Congress adjourned on July 26, 1947 until January 2, 1948, or until earlier convened by certain stated officials of the Senate and House. If Congress is so convened under this latter alternative the amendments become effective three months from the date of its adjournment sine die after being so convened. If Congress is not so convened, the 1946 amendments become effective three months after Congress adjourns after convening on January 2, 1948. Since the second regular session of the 80th Congress begins at noon on January 6, 1948, the first regular session, when it convenes, must of necessity adjourn sine die on January 2, or some intervening day prior to January 6, 1948. The 1946 amendments therefore become effective on or about April 2, 1948 depending upon the actual day when the reconvened 

Congress adjourns.

Applicability to pending actions. By virtue of Rule 86 (b) the 1946 amendments govern "all further proceedings in actions then pending, except to the

¶ 0.01 

extent that in the opinion of the court their application in a particular action pending when the amendments take effect would not be feasible or would work injustice, in which event the former procedure applies." While we have seen the 1946 amendments are quite extensive, they are, nevertheless, not revolutionary. In the main they are merely clarifying, and where the practice has been changed the new practice can be applied to pending actions without prejudice to any of the parties, subject to at least one exception. 

Rule 73(a) has, in general, reduced the time for appeal to a circuit court of appeals from three months, at least in the case of final judgments, to 30 days in private cases and 60 days in government cases. It would not be feasible to apply this rule, in so far as it reduces the time for appeal, to a case where a judgment had been entered prior to the effective date of the amendments, and the time for appeal, if amended Rule 73 (a) were applied, would have run. It is obvious that this would work an injustice. But suppose a judgment had been entered prior to the effective date of the amendments, but the losing party had made a timely motion which affects the finality of the judgment and tolls the running of the time for appeal [see Rule 73 (a)], such as a motion for a new trial, and this motion was still under consideration and was decided by the court after the effective date of the amendments. 

Since the time for appeal was tolled by the motion for a new trial and the full time for appeal commences to run from the order denying the motion for new trial, where this motion is made either before or after the effective date of the amendments, but is decided subsequent thereto, it would not be impractical to say that Rule 73(a) applied so that the 30 or 60 day period, as the case may be, applied. On the other hand, a convenient rule of thumb would be to make amended Rule 73 (a) on appeal time apply only to cases where a judgment is entered after the effective date of the amendments. 

Nevertheless, the careful practitioner will take an appeal, which can be simply done by filing a notice of appeal, within the time period stated in Rule 73(a), from a judgment entered prior to the effective date of the amendments, in the event any of the timely motions enumerated in Rule 73(a) are disposed of after said effective date.

A motion for a new trial on the ground of newly discovered evidence may also present trouble. Suppose a final judgment is entered in private litigation 40 days prior to the effective date of the amendments, and no motion affecting the appeal time is pending at the effective date. Since under the 

Rules, as originally promulgated, the losing party had three months to take an appeal to the circuit court of appeals, while under amended Rule 73 (a) he now has only 30 days, we concluded above that the appeal time of amended Rule 73 (a) could not be applied to this pending action. Now may the district court entertain a motion for new trial on the ground of newly discovered evidence? Under original Rule 59 (b) this motion could be made "before the expiration of the time for appeal, with leave of court obtained on notice and hearing and on a showing of due diligence"; and since under our example the time for appeal has not run, the motion could be entertained under original Rule 59 (b). Under amended Rule 59(b), however, a motion for new trial on any ground must be served not later than 10 days after the entry of the judgment; and relief from a judgment on the basis of new evidence

¶ 0.

174 discovered after the 10 day period is taken care of by amended Rule 60 (b). 

See Comments under Rules 59 and 60, supra. The district court certainly would have discretion to apply original Rule 59 (b) and entertain the motion for new trial on the basis of newly discovered evidence, although if it applied the amendments to the pending action this would not seem to work an injustice since amended Rule 60 (b) would afford the moving party ample relief. 

We have also seen that the 10 day period within which to move for the amendment of findings under Rule 52(b) was subject to enlargement under original Rule 6(b), but is not under an amendment to the latter rule. See 

Comments under Rules 6, and 52, supra. Amended Rule 6(b) should not be applied in this situation where a judgment was entered prior to the effective date of the amendments, and prejudice would result.

There should be a common sense and equitable accommodation of the amended rules to pending actions. Where relief from a judgment, either in the district court or on appeal, is involved, care should be taken that the transition from the former to the new procedure does not prejudice any substantial rights. In other situations there is less danger of prejudice, and where there is no likelihood that important rights will be impaired the amended rules should be applied in pending actions so that the transition over can be effected as speedily as possible.

[2]-[Official] Introductory Statement.
